Psychological Distress at Cues: Intense or prolonged psychological distress at exposure to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Physiological Reactions to Cues: Marked physiological reactions to internal or external cues that symbolize or resemble an aspect of the traumatic event.\n  - Depersonalization: Feeling detached from one's own body or mental processes, as if being an outside observer.\n  - Derealization: The surrounding environment feels unreal, dreamlike, distant, or distorted.\n  - Time Distortion: Time feels like it has stopped during the traumatic moment, or is perceived as moving extremely slowly or quickly.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 2 weeks\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been feeling completely overwhelmed by severe stress and disturbing, intrusive memories of a traumatic event for about two weeks now. My mind constantly feels foggy, and I'm having a really hard time thinking clearly or focusing on anything. It has just become too much to handle on my own.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this traumatic event and whether these symptoms started immediately after it happened or if there was a delay in their onset?\n\nPatient: It's really hard for me to talk about the event itself right now. I haven't forgotten the traumatic event itself, but these strange feelings of detachment and mental fogginess make everything feel very distant.\n\nDoctor: Since your symptoms have been present for about two weeks, can you tell me if you're concerned that these feelings and symptoms might last longer than a month, or do you feel like they might start to improve soon?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how long these symptoms will last, and I haven't thought about whether they'll go on past a month. I'm just hoping I can start feeling better soon.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the traumatic event?\n\nPatient: I don't think I am actively avoiding people, places, or activities right now.     "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"Posttraumatic Stress Disorder\", \"Acute Stress Disorder\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been feeling completely overwhelmed by severe stress and disturbing, intrusive memories of a traumatic event for about two weeks now. My mind constantly feels foggy, and I'm having a really hard time thinking clearly or focusing on anything. It has just become too much to handle on my own.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about this traumatic event and whether these symptoms started immediately after it happened or if there was a delay in their onset?\n\nPatient: It's really hard for me to talk about the event itself right now. I'm not entirely sure about the exact timeline of when the symptoms started, but I've been feeling this way for about two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been continuous over the past two weeks, or have they been changing or evolving in any way since they first started?\n\nPatient: Yes, they have been completely continuous and ongoing. Ever since the event happened two weeks ago, I haven't had any relief or real change in how I feel. It just feels like a constant weight that hasn't let up at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you've been able to function in your daily life, such as going to work or school, or taking care of yourself and others, or have these symptoms significantly impaired your ability to do so?\n\nPatient: My daily functioning has been significantly impaired because I find it almost impossible to focus on basic tasks like reading, watching TV, or even holding a conversation. On top of that, making minor daily decisions like what to wear or what to eat has become completely paralyzing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any dissociative symptoms, such as feeling detached from yourself or the world around you, or have you had any episodes where you've completely forgotten parts of the traumatic event?\n\nPatient: Yes, I often feel detached, like I'm an outside observer watching my own body, and my surroundings frequently seem completely unreal and dreamlike. I haven't forgotten the traumatic event itself, but these strange feelings of detachment and mental fogginess make everything feel very distant.\n\nDoctor: Since your symptoms have been present for about two weeks, can you tell me if you're concerned that these feelings and symptoms might last longer than a month, or do you feel like they might start to improve soon?\n\nPatient: I'm really not sure how long these symptoms will last, and I haven't thought about whether they'll go on past a month. I'm just hoping I can start feeling better soon.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding people, places, or activities that remind you of the traumatic event?\n\nPatient: I don't think I am actively avoiding people, places, or activities right now. I haven't really noticed myself doing that, as I've mostly just been trying to get through each day with how overwhelmed I feel.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe any specific physical or emotional reactions you experience when you're reminded of the traumatic event, such as increased heart rate, sweating, or feelings of intense fear or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, whenever I am reminded of the event, I experience intense anxiety and a deep sense of fear. Physically, my heart immediately starts racing, and I begin to sweat. It is a very overwhelming reaction that makes me feel like I'm right back in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your symptoms and reactions to reminders of the traumatic event are likely to persist beyond a month, or do you anticipate they will begin to diminish soon?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know if these feelings will persist or if they will start to improve in the future. I just know that I have been dealing with them constantly for about two weeks now, and it's hard to imagine how things will go.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else you'd like to share or discuss about your experience, or would you like me to summarize what we've talked about so far and discuss potential next steps?\n\nPatient: Yes, please, I would really appreciate it if you could summarize what we've discussed and talk about the next steps. My mind feels very foggy and tired right now, so having you lay it all out clearly would be incredibly helpful."

The presence of intense anxiety, fear, and physical reactions to reminders of the traumatic event supports both Posttraumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD) and Acute Stress Disorder (ASD). Since the symptoms have not yet exceeded the one-month duration typically required for a PTSD diagnosis, and given the patient's uncertainty about the future persistence of symptoms, both PTSD and ASD remain as candidates. The patient's experience of dissociative symptoms and the impact on daily life also aligns with criteria for both disorders. Further evaluation is needed to determine if symptoms will persist beyond a month, which would be more indicative of PTSD, or if they will begin to resolve, potentially aligning more with ASD.", "is_final": false}

Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.
